=== BUGGY CODE ===
class Solution:
    def stoneGameVI(self, a: List[int], b: List[int]) -> int:
        # hurt other enemy rather than self collecting to max
        combines = [(a[i] + b[i], a[i], b[i]) for i in range(len(a))]
        combines.sort(reverse=True) # why we can sort? -> because we can pick any stone we want
        bobPoints = sum(b)
        alicePoints = 0
        for i in range(1, len(a), 2):
            alicePoints += combines[i][1]
            bobPoints -= combines[i][2]
        if alicePoints > bobPoints:
            return 1
        elif alicePoints < bobPoints:
            return -1
        return 0

Observe the following faulty python code. It contains one or more bugs.
Fix the code and output only the fixed code.

```python
class Solution:
    def stoneGameVI(self, a: List[int], b: List[int]) -> int:
        # hurt other enemy rather than self collecting to max
        combines = [(a[i] + b[i], a[i], b[i]) for i in range(len(a))]
        combines.sort(reverse=True) # why we can sort? -> because we can pick any stone we want
        bobPoints = sum(b)
        alicePoints = 0
        for i in range(1, len(a), 2):
            alicePoints += combines[i][1]
            bobPoints -= combines[i][2]
        if alicePoints > bobPoints:
            return 1
        elif alicePoints < bobPoints:
            return -1
        return 0
```

Reason in a repair-and-recheck way.

A practical pattern for this task usually looks like this:
- Start from the clearest failing symptom, suspicious line, or local inconsistency.
- Build the smallest explanation that accounts for that symptom, then check it against the nearby control flow, state updates, and dependent conditions.
- Once one repair path is reasonably supported, move into corrected code early and prefer a fix that can be checked against the same failing path.
- After the edit, rerun that path or the most relevant edge case.
- Then do one small coupled-region scan: a directly connected guard, index update, return path, or call site that could still break the same fix.
- If the recheck is still off, refine the same local region before opening a distant new theory.
- Once the failing path and its directly coupled region look consistent, finalize the code.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- staying in diagnosis mode for too long without testing the next plausible edit;
- moving from a local repair into a fresh branch of speculative causes when the current path has not been rechecked yet;
- turning a focused fix into a broad rewrite unless the surrounding code clearly forces it.

from typing import List

class Solution:
    def stoneGameVI(self, a: List[int], b: List[int]) -> int:
        combines = [(a[i] + b[i], a[i], b[i]) for i in range(len(a))]
        combines.sort(reverse=True)
        bobPoints = sum(b)
        alicePoints = 0
        for i in range(0, len(a), 2):
            alicePoints += combines[i][1]
            bobPoints -= combines[i][2]
        if alicePoints > bobPoints:
            return 1
        elif alicePoints < bobPoints:
            return -1
        return 0

from typing import List

class Solution:
    def stoneGameVI(self, a: List[int], b: List[int]) -> int:
        combines = [(a[i] + b[i], a[i], b[i]) for i in range(len(a))]
        combines.sort(reverse=True)

        bobPoints = sum(b)
        alicePoints = 0

        for i in range(0, len(a), 2):
            alicePoints += combines[i][1]
            bobPoints -= combines[i][2]

        if alicePoints > bobPoints:
            return 1
        elif alicePoints < bobPoints:
            return -1
        return 0

But if the problem's official solution uses `sort(key=lambda x: x[0]+x[1], reverse=True)`, tuple sort is accepted. Let's maybe search memory: LeetCode 1686 Python solution:
class Solution:
    def stoneGameVI(self, a: List[int], b: List[int]) -> int:
        s = [(a[i]+b[i], a[i], b[i]) for i in range(len(a))]
        s.sort(reverse=True)
        alice = sum(x[1] for x in s[::2])
        bob = sum(x[2] for x in s[1::2])
        return (alice > bob) - (alice < bob)
Actually many use tuple sort. So okay.

Let's derive general. For a group of equal sum s, let A be set Alice gets, B Bob gets. Contribution = sum_{i in A} a_i - sum_{j in B} b_j. Since b_j = s - a_j, contribution = sum_A a_i - (|B|s - sum_B a_j) = sum_{A∪B} a_i - |B|s. This is independent of which stones are in A/B, only |B|. The number of stones Bob gets from the group is determined by the turn order and group length, not by tie order. So yes. Good.
